# Corporate responses to generative AI: early evidence from conference calls

Ning Jia $ ^{1} $ $ ^{ID} $ · Ningzhong Li $ ^{2} $ · Guang Ma $ ^{3} $ · Da Xu $ ^{1} $

Accepted: 5 August 2025

## Abstract

© The Author(s), under exclusive licence to Springer Science+Business Media, LLC, part of Springer Nature 2025

We provide early evidence of corporate responses to generative artificial intelligence (GAI) through managerial discussions of GAI in conference calls after ChatGPT’s release in November 2022. Following the release, these managerial discussions increase substantially, and most discussions have positive tone and contain action initiatives. The increase is more pronounced for larger firms, growth firms, younger firms, and those in high-tech industries. The largest increase is observed in the industry of business equipment—computers, software, and electronic equipment—while the utilities, chemicals and allied products, and oil, gas, and coal extraction industries experience the smallest increases. Managers of firms with greater innovation intensity (reflected in patent filings), cybersecurity threats, labor exposure to AI, and customer operations are more likely to increase GAI discussions. The results are primarily driven by positive-tone discussions and those with action initiatives. Analyst discussions of GAI exhibit similar patterns, except for patent filings. Finally, managers’ GAI discussions inform investors.

Keywords Generative AI · ChatGPT · Conference call · Financial analysts · Market reaction

JEL Classification K4 · M4

## 1 Introduction

Disruptive technologies can reshape industries and markets, and generative artificial intelligence (GAI) appears to be no exception. Marked by the release of ChatGPT in November 2022, GAI uses machine learning algorithms and natural language processing to allow users to have human-like conversations with the technology and to create verbal and graphic content. ChatGPT quickly gained popularity, attracting over 1 million registered users in just five days and 100 million users in two months. $ ^{1} $ It has also drawn wide attention to GAI from the public and the business community. Surveyed CEOs believe that GAI will revolutionize how some companies operate (Deloitte 2023).

GAI presents opportunities and threats for firms (McKinsey 2024), which likely vary with industry and firm characteristics. Thus, it is worthwhile to understand how different kinds of firms respond to GAI. We thus explore how managerial discussions of GAI in conference calls change following the release of ChatGPT. We assume that managers are aware of GAI-induced opportunities and threats after ChatGPT's public release and that, if they perceive their firms to be potentially affected, they are likely to talk more about GAI in conference calls. Moreover, if they perceive GAI to represent primarily opportunities (threats) to their firm, they are likely to discuss GAI with a positive (negative) tone.

Conference calls are a preferred setting for this analysis for two key reasons. First, the calls are less formal and regulated (Calomiris et al. 2023) than other forms of disclosure, and thus managers may be franker about their views on GAI than they are in other disclosures. $ ^{2} $ Consistent with this conjecture, we find that discussions of GAI in 10-Ks are rare in the year following the release of ChatGPT. Second, compared to 10-Ks, conference calls are interactive, so that call participants can ask questions about GAI, even if managers do not voluntarily share their views (Armstrong et al. 2025).

We first provide descriptive evidence on the frequency, intensity, and features (sentiment and whether the discussion includes an action initiative) of managers' GAI discussions in the post-ChatGPT period and compare them with those in the pre-period. We then examine how changes in managerial discussions of GAI (intensity and features) after the launch of ChatGPT vary cross-sectionally with firm and industry characteristics and whether and how these changes inform investors.

Our analyses are based on a sample of 6,192 conference calls hosted by 2,025 public firms between November 30, 2022, and May 27, 2023. To identify GAI discussions, we first search the entire conference call transcript for GAI-related keywords (see Sect. 3.2), and then, for each keyword hit, we manually read sentences around the keyword to extract all GAI-related sentences. The percentage of conference calls

containing GAI discussions increases sharply after the launch of ChatGPT, from 0.34% in the second quarter of 2022 to 9.25% in the second quarter of 2023, indicating that managers promptly respond to this major milestone.

To measure the discussion sentiment, we analyze managers' tone and classify it as either positive, negative, or neutral. Among the post-ChatGPT calls in which managers discuss GAI, 77% have a positive tone, while 10% have a negative tone and 13% have a neutral tone. When discussing GAI, managers may disclose action initiatives or merely make broad remarks (e.g., Balakrishnan et al. 2023). We use ChatGPT-4 to identify whether a call's GAI discussions contain GAI-related initiatives and manually verify the results using a random sample of 100 firms. In 87% of the post-ChatGPT calls in which managers discuss GAI, they disclose GAI-related initiatives. Coupling managers' positive tone with their specific plans suggests that they perceive GAI as an opportunity.

We measure the intensity of managers' GAI discussions in conference calls with the percentage of GAI-related sentences in managers' presentations and answers to analysts' questions. To capture the increase in GAI discussions, we subtract the average of the same percentage measure among conference calls of the same firm in the year before the release of ChatGPT. We find that the increase is more pronounced for firms in high-tech industries, larger firms, growth firms, and younger firms. The largest increase is observed in the Fama–French-12 industry of business equipment—computers, software, and electronic equipment—and the smallest increases are in the utilities, chemicals and allied products, and oil, gas, and coal extraction industries.

We further explore how the change of managers’ GAI discussions is associated with several important firm and industry characteristics related to the ways GAI is believed to impact firms, including innovation, cybersecurity, labor, and customers (e.g., Deloitte 2023; McKinsey 2023). Specifically, we examine firm innovation intensity, cybersecurity threats, labor exposure to AI, and customer operations. As we explain in Sect. 4.1, it is unclear how these firm and industry characteristics influence managers’ GAI discussions. While GAI presents opportunities for corporate innovation (Bilgram and Laarmann 2023; Henry 2023), it is unclear how much managers perceive it as benefiting innovative firms. GAI could either help laggards catch up to innovators or benefit innovators by extending their advantages. Regarding cybersecurity, while GAI provides opportunities to increase protection efficiency and improve threat detection (Campbell 2023), it can also help attackers launch sophisticated and novel attacks (Irei 2023).

While GAI is generally perceived to offer opportunities to improve firm productivity by substituting for labor and increasing labor productivity (McKinsey 2023; Brynjolfsson et al. 2025), it also can threaten firms. Many employees have concerns about being replaced by GAI, which could harm their psychological welfare, lessen their organizational commitment, and decrease their incentives for human capital investment (Dekker and Schaufeli 1995; Brougham and Haar 2018). Similarly, the impact of GAI on customer operations is also double-edged: while it can improve customer operations (Brynjolfsson et al. 2025), it could also introduce risks, such as providing false information and reducing compassion or empathy (Froehlich 2023).

Empirically, to capture the different aspects of innovation, we measure a firm’s innovation intensity by its patent applications, trade secrecy discussions in 10-Ks,

and research and development (R&D) expenditure (Gustavo 2011; Fang et al. 2014; Glaeser et al. 2023; Dyer et al. 2024; Glaeser and Lang 2024). Cybersecurity threat is measured with a cyber-risk metric constructed based on the textual analysis of a firm's 10-K filings (Florackis et al. 2023). We capture the degree of labor exposure to AI using the measure of industry-level occupational exposure to AI developed by Felten et al. (2021) and the intensity of customer operations using whether a firm primarily operates in the retail or service sector (Verma 2000; Wang 2024).

We find that, following the release of ChatGPT, firms with more patent applications, greater cybersecurity threats, higher labor exposure to AI, and more customer operations experience a larger increase in managers' GAI discussions in subsequent conference calls. These results imply that managers of those firms perceive their firms to be more affected by GAI. A one-standard-deviation increase in patents (cybersecurity threat, labor exposure to AI) is associated with a greater increase in managers' GAI discussions that accounts for 32% (26%, 37%) of the sample average increase. Industries with more customer operations are associated with a greater increase in managers' GAI discussions that accounts for 268% of the sample average increase. In contrast, neither trade secrecy discussions nor R&D expenditure are significantly associated with the change of managers' GAI discussions. These results suggest that innovation inputs are insignificantly associated with changes in managers' GAI discussions, and among innovation outputs (patents and trade secrets), only patents show a significant association. This may be because patents are better legally protected, allowing the firm to innovate without fearing appropriation by competitors (Hall et al. 2014). We also examine analysts' perceptions of GAI's impact, as reflected in their discussions during the Q&A sections of calls. We find that their perceptions resemble those of managers, except that changes in their discussions are insignificantly associated with patent filings.

Separately exploring changes of GAI discussions with positive, negative, and neutral sentiments, we find that managers of firms with more patents or greater cybersecurity threats increase their positive discussions of GAI more but not their negative or neutral ones. These findings suggest that managers generally believe that GAI will benefit firms with more patent filings and cybersecurity threats, presumably because it can enhance their innovation and cybersecurity. For firms operating in industries with higher labor exposure to AI and more customer operations, managers' views appear to be mixed—they are more likely to increase GAI discussions of all three tones. These findings support our earlier arguments that GAI could either help or hurt these firms. However, the results for positive-tone discussions are stronger than those for negative-tone ones, suggesting that the perspectives of managers of those firms are more likely to be positive than negative. The results based on analysts' discussions are similar, except that patent filings are insignificantly associated with changes in positive-tone discussions.

We also explore how the four firm and industry characteristics are associated with changes of managers' GAI discussions with and without initiatives. We find that managers at firms with more patents, greater cybersecurity threats, and more customer operations increase initiative-related discussions of GAI more but not general discussions. Managers of firms in industries with higher labor exposure to AI increase both types of discussions, with stronger results for initiative-related discus-

sions. The results based on analysts’ discussions are similar, except that patents are insignificantly associated with changes of initiative-related discussions.

Next we find that larger increases in GAI discussions (intensity, discussions with positive tone, and initiative-related discussions) are associated with stronger market reactions and higher abnormal trading volume, and firms with higher institutional ownership primarily drive the responses. Furthermore, the results for initiative-related discussions strengthen when the discussions are more specific and certain. This evidence suggests that managers' GAI discussions inform investors.

Lastly, we explore how the four firm and industry characteristics are associated with the market reaction to the launch of ChatGPT, which reflects investors' perceptions of GAI's impacts. We find that the market reaction is positively and significantly associated with patent filings and cybersecurity threats, which is consistent with the findings based on managers' GAI discussions. However, unlike the findings from manager discussions, the market reaction is negatively and significantly associated with R&D expenditure and trade secrets and is insignificantly associated with labor exposure to AI or customer operations. These results suggest that investors' perceptions of GAI can differ from those of managers (and analysts). This justifies separately examining managers' perceptions (as well as analysts') on top of research based on market reactions (e.g., Eisfeldt et al. 2023).

A potential concern is that managers’ GAI discussions in conference calls may reflect “AI washing”—making false, misleading, or exaggerated statements about whether and how firms are using or developing AI systems (e.g., Barrios et al. 2025). For example, some initiatives discussed may be superficial from an economic perspective. We provide some analyses to mitigate this concern but acknowledge that we cannot rule it out and leave further exploration of this issue for future research.

Our paper adds to the nascent literature on the corporate impacts of GAI. Given the revolutionary nature of ChatGPT, it is unsurprising that scholars as well as practitioners have tried to understand its impact. Concurrent studies have examined its overall valuation impact (e.g., Eisfeldt et al. 2023) and productivity impact in specific settings (e.g., Gilardi et al. 2023; Yilmaz et al. 2023; Brynjolfsson et al. 2025). $ ^{3} $ We add to this literature by providing systematic evidence on how and under what circumstances managers discuss GAI. We provide detailed evidence on the frequency, intensity, and features of managers' GAI discussions right after ChatGPT's public launch and compare them with the pre-period. We also examine how changes in GAI discussions vary with industry membership and firm characteristics. More importantly, we show how the discussion changes are associated with firm and industry characteristics related to the major ways GAI is perceived to impact firms. Our evidence based on analysts' GAI discussions complements our findings and inferences about corporate responses.

Overall, our study provides early insights into how managers, analysts, investors, and other stakeholders perceive GAI's potential influences. Our findings suggest that perceptions of GAI may vary among stakeholders. Once more data on firms' actual use of GAI become available, future research could assess the real impacts of GAI and determine whether they align with the currently documented perceptions.

## 2 Background, literature review, and conceptual framework

### 2.1 Background of generative AI

Adopting AI for human activities has a long history. In 1954 IBM made the first public demonstration of a machine translation system (Zhang et al. 2023). However, it was not until November 2022 that AI captured the global attention with the introduction a generative AI product, ChatGPT, by OpenAI. This GAI application emerged from foundation models—large-scale, deep learning models trained on massive structured and unstructured datasets (text, voice, etc.) covering many topics. Unlike traditional AI tools designed to summarize patterns and make predictions, GAI can create content for a broad range of uses with little fine-tuning required for each case. For example, ChatGPT has been used to translate text and to create protein sequences. Its capabilities are accessible to all, including people with little computer literacy.

Due to its wide applications and low usage barrier, GAI is widely perceived to significantly affect society and economy. It has the potential to reshape work across the economy (McKinsey 2023). As a result, the release of ChatGPT has drawn public attention to this product and to GAI. Figure 1 presents the trend of worldwide Google search interest for the phrase “ChatGPT,” “GPT,” and “generative AI/generative artificial intelligence.” Search interest surged sharply after the release of ChatGPT and grew until the end of our examination window, June 2023.

GAI has been attracting the interest of investors and corporations. The funding for GAI has swelled, reaching $12 billion in the first five months of 2023 alone (McKinsey 2023). More companies are investing in their own GAI products. Among the

This figure presents the time-series trend of worldwide Google search interest index for the phrase "ChatGPT," "GPT," and "Generative AI/Generative Artificial Intelligence" between June 2022 and June 2023</div> </div>


major players, Microsoft has reportedly invested $10 billion in OpenAI and Chat-GPT. In December 2023, Google introduced its GAI product, Gemini. Despite the fast development of GAI, this technology is not without challenges and limitations. For example, the accuracy of the results can be questionable. GAI tools can generate inaccuracies in response to users' questions and have no way to signal this to the user. Another concern is systemic biases in results, as GAI tools may be trained on data that include biases (Chilton 2023).

### 2.2 Literature

Given the significance of GAI, an emerging literature has begun to examine its impacts on businesses, especially following the launch of ChatGPT. Eisfeldt et al. (2023) focus on the valuation implications of a particular type of firm exposure to GAI—workforce exposure. They measure this exposure by analyzing job tasks and their susceptibility to AI-driven automation or enhancement, creating an index that quantifies exposure across a company's workforce. Using a portfolio strategy, they find that higher-exposure firms earn higher excess returns compared to lower-exposure ones. They conclude that the release of ChatGPT is generally viewed by investors as good news for more exposed firms. However, they also find that investors' perceptions vary across and within industries.

A stream of research examines the productivity impact of GAI, usually focusing on how GAI performs compared to humans and how it can improve labor productivity in a specific setting (e.g., Kreitmeir and Raschky 2023; Noy and Zhang 2023; Eulerich et al. 2024). Bertomeu et al. (2025) examine how GAI facilitates financial analysts' information processing using the Italian ban on ChatGPT as an exogenous shock. They find that the ban diminishes analyst forecast accuracy and market efficiency. Yilmaz et al. (2023) use Google's introduction of neural network-based translation (GNNT), a type of GAI-based application, as a natural experiment to examine the substitution of human translators by AI. They find that the introduction of GNNT reduces the number of human translation transactions. Brynjolfsson et al. (2025) examine the impact of GAI-based tools on labor productivity in customer support activities. They find that customer support agents using AI tools resolve more customer issues per hour. Using a sample of tweets, Gilardi et al. (2023) demonstrate that GAI outperforms crowd-workers for several text-annotation tasks.

### 2.3 Conceptual framework

We aim to add to the understanding of GAI’s impacts by examining how firms responded to GAI based on managers’ GAI discussions in conference calls. Research has shown that conference calls provide insights beyond traditional disclosures, with much of their informativeness attributed to their interactive and sometimes unscripted nature (Frankel et al. 1999; Matsumoto et al. 2011; Lee 2016). $ ^{4} $ Therefore, the literature has used conference calls and corporate disclosure broadly to measure

managerial perceptions of various issues, such as corporate culture (Li et al. 2021), political risk (Hassan et al. 2019), Brexit uncertainty (Hassan et al. 2024a), epidemic exposure (Hassan et al. 2023), and cybersecurity risk (Jamilov et al. 2021). This literature assumes that managers' discussions in conference calls, on average, reflect their views and corporate actions. For example, Balakrishnan et al. (2023) use conference calls to measure a firm's exposure to diversity issues and whether the firm tries to advance its diversity-related efforts. Hassan et al. (2024b) use conference calls held by listed firms around the world to measure the amount of risk that managers and investors perceive in each country.

We use a similar framework, assuming that managers’ GAI discussions reflect their firms’ responses to the technology. The responses in managers’ discussions may include their real actions taken, their plans, or their views without any actions or plans. We assume that these responses stem from managers’ perceptions of how GAI may affect their firms, which may vary with firm and industry characteristics, such as innovation intensity and cybersecurity threats. If managers perceive their firms to be potentially affected by GAI, they are likely to talk more about GAI. If they perceive GAI to represent primarily opportunities (threats) to their firm, they are likely to discuss GAI with a positive (negative) tone.

One concern is that managers' discussions of GAI during conference calls may involve AI washing. Several factors help mitigate this concern. Litigation risks and the need to adhere to securities regulations discourage managers from providing materially misleading information (Heitzman et al. 2010; Huang et al. 2020). Market discipline and reputation concern further deter misrepresentation (Healy and Palepu 2001). The sophistication of analysts and institutional investors, who are skilled in identifying inconsistencies, adds scrutiny (Lang and Lundholm 1993). Finally, as discussed above, the real-time nature of conference calls also helps ensure accountability. Managers must answer questions immediately, and follow-up inquiries can expose their evasions. This dynamic may impede managers from biasing or exaggerating information. We provide further discussion of AI washing in Sect. 4.3.

## 3 Sample selection, measurement of GAI discussions, and descriptive evidence

### 3.1 Sample selection

Our sample comprises publicly Listed companies on the NYSE and Nasdaq, with financial data accessible from Compustat. Initially, we consider the universe of 8,065 companies in Compustat, all of which have reported positive total assets for the fiscal year ending between November 29, 2021, and November 30, 2022 (the public launch date of ChatGPT). After requiring availability of firm variables, our sample shrinks to 2,869 firms. We search for firms' conference call transcripts in Capital IQ Transcript Database and retrieve 6,772 transcripts for our sample firms, spanning the period of November 30, 2022, to May 27, 2023. After excluding transcripts without conference call variables, we are left with a sample of 6,192 transcripts for 2,025 firms. For the stock return analysis, we further require the availability of stock prices from Center

for Research in Security Prices (CRSP). The sample selection procedures are detailed in Appendix A.

### 3.2 Measurement of GAI discussions

To identify GAI discussions in conference calls, we first compile a list of GAI-related keywords along three dimensions—vendor, product, and technology. We posit that managers are unlikely to discuss GAI without mentioning any of these three dimensions. For vendor and product-related keywords, we identify leading GAI vendors by referencing several commercial rankings, and for each vendor, we obtain information about its product offerings from its website. $ ^{5} $ For technology keywords, we first extract a preliminary list of keywords from Zhang et al. (2023), a computer science survey paper on GAI technology. We then ask three technology experts to independently select the keywords they perceive as highly relevant to GAI. $ ^{6} $ We retain keywords that at least two choose. The final keyword list is provided in Appendix B.

For each conference call transcript, we search for the GAI-related keywords in the entire transcript and identify sentences that contain the keywords. $ ^{7} $ We then manually read sentences around them to identify all GAI discussions. Hence, our final GAI-related sentences consist of both the keyword sentences and the context. We provide examples of these discussions in Appendix C.

#### 3.2.1 GAI discussion intensity

To measure the intensity of managers’ GAI discussions, we compute the percentage of GAI-related sentences relative to the aggregate sentence count in management discourses—the presentations and answers to analysts’ questions and denote this variable as GAI%. To capture the change of GAI discussions following ChatGPT’s release, we subtract the average of GAI% among conference calls of the same firm between November 30, 2021, and November 29, 2022, and denote the change as  $ \Delta GAI\% $. We use a similar approach to identify analysts’ GAI discussions based on analysts’ questions during the call Q&A. The corresponding variables are denoted as GAI%-Analyst and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Analyst.

#### 3.2.2 GAI discussion sentiment

To measure the tone of managers’ GAI discussions, we classify the net sentiment of the discussions in each conference call as positive, negative, and neutral. We use the lexicon introduced by Loughran and McDonald (2011) to ascertain the net sentiment of a call’s GAI discussions. A call with GAI discussions is categorized as having positive GAI discussions if the net sentiment is positive (above zero), neutral if the net sentiment equals zero, and negative if the net sentiment is negative (below zero). We present one example of each kind of discussion in Appendix C Panel A.

After measuring the net sentiment of a call's GAI discussions, we decompose GAI% into GAI%-Pos, GAI%-Neg, and GAI%-Neu. GAI%-Pos equals GAI% if the net sentiment of GAI discussions is positive and zero otherwise. GAI%-Neg and GAI%-Neu are defined similarly. By construction, GAI% is the sum of the three sentiment-based measures. We use this holistic approach (rather than measuring each sentence's sentiment) because it better reflects managers' overall perception of GAI. We then calculate the change of GAI%-Pos by subtracting the average GAI%-Pos of the same firm in the pre-ChatGPT period (between November 30, 2021, and November 29, 2022) and denote the change as  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Pos. We define  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Neg and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Neu similarly. We use a similar approach to construct the corresponding variables for analysts and denote them by adding the suffix -Analyst to the manager variables.

#### 3.2.3 Initiative-related versus other GAI discussions

We use ChatGPT-4 to identify whether a call's GAI discussions contain GAI-related initiatives and manually verify the results using a random sample of 100 firms. $ ^{8} $ ChatGPT-4 performs fairly well in distinguishing the two kinds of discussions, achieving a machine-human consistency rate of 86%. $ ^{9} $ Appendix C Panel B provides one example of each type. After the classification, we follow a similar procedure as in Sect. 3.2.1 to calculate GAI discussions with initiatives, GAI%-Initiative, and discussions without, GAI%-NonInitiative, and their changes,  $ \Delta GAI\%-Initiative $ and  $ \Delta GAI\%-NonInitiative $. We use a similar approach to construct the corresponding variables for analysts (whether they ask about GAI initiatives) and denote them by adding the suffix -Analyst to the manager variables.

27 years old and has a market-to-book ratio of 6.449, 13 analysts following it, and 76% institutional ownership. About half (48.6%) of firms are in high-tech industries. Panel B also reports the statistics on firm characteristics related to innovation, cybersecurity, labor, and customer as well as market reaction-related variables.

### 4.1 Descriptive evidence on managers' GAI discussions

#### 4.1.1 Frequency, intensity, features, and changes

Panel A of Table 1 shows the average intensity and features of managers' GAI discussions and their changes. Regarding discussion intensity, the mean of GAI% is 0.316, indicating that, in the post-ChatGPT period, 0.316% of managers' discussions in conference calls relate to GAI. The mean of  $ \Delta GAI\% $ is 0.306, which means, relative to the benchmark period of one year before the launch of ChatGPT, GAI discussions increase by 0.306 percentage points on average. The difference between GAI% and  $ \Delta GAI\% $ indicates that the average value of GAI% in the pre-ChatGPT period is only 0.01 percentage points. Thus, managers rarely discussed GAI before ChatGPT, and their discussions increase sharply following its public launch.

To provide further insight into the time-series trend of managers’ GAI discussions, in Fig. 2A, we present the quarterly distribution of the percentage of conference calls that contain GAI discussions over the fourth quarter of 2021 to the second quarter of

<div style="text-align: center;"><img src="https://pplines-online.bj.bcebos.com/deploy/official/paddleocr/pp-ocr-vl-15//c5fea7fb-d57d-4b18-a7be-8704974ff9c3/markdown_2/imgs/img_in_chart_box_108_106_771_438.jpg?authorization=bce-auth-v1%2FALTAKzReLNvew3ySINYJ0fuAMN%2F2026-04-04T15%3A01%3A20Z%2F-1%2F%2F0a8488645f722221dc1f88db7b3c9ed2203c5445435f8962f809d530af38f555" alt="Image" width="75%" /></div>

<div style="text-align: center;"><div style="text-align: center;">Fig. 2 GAI Discussions in conference calls over time. A presents the percentage of conference calls in which managers discuss GAI for each quarter between the fourth quarter of 2021 and the second quarter of 2023. B presents the mean of the measure of managers' GAI discussion intensity GAI% (defined in Appendix D) for each quarter between the fourth quarter of 2021 and the second quarter of 2023.</div> </div>


2023. Prior to 2023, on average only less than 1% of calls contained GAI discussions. The percentage surged sharply to around 9% in the second quarter of 2023, consistent with the statistics reported in Table 1. Figure 2B presents the quarterly distribution of GAI%. Its increasing trend resembles that in Fig. 2A.

Regarding the sentiment of managers’ GAI discussions, Panel A of Table 1 reports that the means of GAI%-Pos, GAI%-Neg, and GAI%-Neu are 0.263, 0.030, and 0.019, respectively. The average values of GAI%-Initiative and GAI%-NonInitiative are 0.305 and 0.012, respectively. As the mean of GAI% is 0.316, these statistics suggest that managers’ GAI discussions mostly have positive net sentiment and contain

action initiatives. $ ^{10} $ This indicates that managers of firms likely affected by GAI generally view it as presenting opportunities to their firms and are inclined to capitalize on these opportunities.

For comparison, we also report the descriptive statistics for analysts' GAI discussions in conference calls in Table 1 Panel A, which show similar patterns and features as managers' discussions. Specifically, analysts rarely discuss GAI before ChatGPT, and their discussions increase substantially following its release. In the post-period, when analysts discuss GAI, they typically express positive views and ask about action initiatives. Thus, it seems that analysts' perceptions of GAI, on average, resemble those of managers. Consistent with this observation, the correlation between GAI% and GAI%-Analyst is high (0.64), and so is that between  $ \Delta GAI\% $ and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Analyst (also 0.64).

#### 4.1.2 Cross-industry variation

We explore the cross-industry (Fama–French-12) variation of changes of managers’ GAI discussions (intensity and features) in Fig. 3. As managers rarely discuss GAI before ChatGPT, the figures based on the level of managers’ GAI discussions in the post-period are very similar and thus omitted. Figure 3A presents the variation for changes of discussion intensity ( $ \Delta GAI\% $). While on average managers’ GAI discussions increase (Table 1), there are significant variations across industries. The Fama–French-12 industry of business equipment—computers, software, and electronic equipment—experiences the largest increase in managers’ GAI discussions, and the increase exceeds that in any other industry. The mean of  $ \Delta GAI\% $ in the business equipment industry is 1.067, while those of the other industries fall below 0.2. The three industries with the least increase in managers’ GAI discussions are utilities, chemicals and allied products, and oil, gas, and coal extraction with the means of  $ \Delta GAI\% $ of 0, 0, and 0.003, respectively. Managers in these industries almost never discuss GAI before or after ChatGPT’s arrival. This cross-industry pattern is somewhat expected because high-tech industries are more exposed to automation and optimization through GAI (McKinsey 2024). In contrast, industries like utilities rely more on capital investments, physical processes, and infrastructure and thus are probably viewed by managers as less affected by GAI.

Figure 3B separately presents the changes of managers' GAI discussions with different sentiment. For industries with nontrivial discussion changes, the relative changes of positive-, negative-, and neutral-tone discussions are generally consistent with the general pattern reported in Panel A of Table 1—the increased discussions are predominantly driven by positive-tone discussions while negative- and neutral-tone discussions also increase, with two exceptions. First, the increase in positive-tone discussions is smaller than those in negative- and neutral-tone discussions in the consumer durables industry. Second, there are no changes of negative-tone discussions.

in two industries: manufacturing and telephone and television transmission. It seems in managers' views, the influence of GAI in the consumer durables industry is more likely to be negative relative to an average industry, and it is more likely to be positive in the manufacturing and telephone and television transmission industries.

Figure 3C separately presents changes of managers’ GAI discussions with and without initiatives. For industries with nontrivial discussion changes, the relative

changes of initiative- versus other discussions are generally consistent with the pattern reported in Panel A of Table 1—the increased discussions are predominantly driven by discussions of initiatives, with notable exception of the telephone and television transmission industry, which has no discussions of initiatives.

We use Fama–French-12 industries in Fig. 3 to save figure space. To provide a more granular comparison, we categorize two-digit SIC industries into high-tech (SIC codes 28, 25, 36, 73, and 87) and non-high-tech ones (the other SIC codes), following Kimbrough and Louis (2011) and De Franco et al. (2023). Table 2 Panel A reports that the increases of managers' GAI discussions, overall intensity as well as features (except the ones without initiatives), are all significantly higher in high-tech industries. This pattern is consistent with Fig. 3. $ ^{11} $

#### 4.1.3 Variation based on fundamental firm characteristics

In Panels B to D of Table 2, we further compare firms with different fundamental characteristics, including size, age, and growth potential (market-to-book ratio). The sample partition is based on the sample median of each partitioning variable, namely, Size, Firm Age, and Market to Book, which are defined in Appendix D. We find that the increases in managers' GAI discussions, overall as well as those with different features, are generally more pronounced for larger firms, growth firms, and younger firms. $ ^{12} $

These patterns are consistent with economic intuition because these firms arguably have more resources, cost advantages, incentives, or flexibility to incorporate the new GAI technology. For example, larger firms generally have more resources; they also have strong incentives to invest in new technologies to achieve long-term benefits. Younger firms and growth firms emphasize innovation and growth; they are also more agile and willing to adopt new technologies. $ ^{13} $

## 5 Analysis of the economic determinants of GAI discussions

### 5.1 Conceptual discussion and measurement of key variables

The resource-based view of a firm posits that heterogeneity in firm performance is rooted in variation in resources and the ability to achieve and sustain a competitive advantage (Barney 1991; Wernerfelt 1984). From this perspective, we posit that GAI will likely impact firm performance through its impacts on corporate resources and capabilities. Based on economic intuitions, anecdotes, and practitioner views

(e.g., Deloitte 2023; McKinsey 2023), we explore four key dimensions—innovation, cybersecurity, labor, and customers. We provide in Appendix C Panel C examples of GAI discussion for the four dimensions we examine. We posit that, if managers perceive their firms to be potentially affected in a given dimension, they are likely to increase GAI discussions and that if managers perceive GAI to represent primarily opportunities (threats) to their firm in a given dimension, they are likely to discuss GAI with a positive (negative) tone.

#### 5.1.1 Innovation

GAI can benefit corporate innovation (Deloitte 2023; McKinsey 2023). For example, it can accelerate exploration, facilitate prototyping, help researchers brainstorm, and automate routine tasks, such as data analysis, report generation, and content creation (Reed 2022; Bilgram and Laarmann 2023; Henry 2023). Empirically, we explore the association between firm innovation intensity and changes of managers' GAI discussions following the release of ChatGPT. It is unclear whether GAI's innovation benefits are more valuable for firms with greater or less innovation. On one hand, GAI could help less innovative firms to catch up to more innovative ones. On the other, GAI may enable innovators to enhance their innovation advantages.

Glaeser and Lang (2024) note that no single dominant empirical measure captures corporate innovation; each measure has advantages and limitations. Therefore, we employ two commonly used output-based measures—patents (Patents) and trade secrets (Trade Secret)—and one input-based measure—R&D expenditure (R&D) (e.g., Gustavo 2011; Glaeser et al. 2020; Glaeser and Landsman 2021). $ ^{14} $ While innovation inputs such as R&D expenditures better capture firms' investment in innovation, the outcomes of these investments could be uncertain. Among the innovation outputs, patents are better legally protected than trade secrets (Hall et al. 2014; Glaeser and Lang 2024).

To calculate Patents, we count the total number of patent applications submitted to the United States Patent and Trademark Office (USPTO) by a firm and its subsidiaries in the past three calendar years. To address the skewness of the data, we use the inverse hyperbolic sine transformation, which adjusts for skewness and is well defined for nonpositive values (e.g., Burbidge et al. 1988; Glaeser and Omartian 2022). We follow Glaeser (2018) and use trade secret discussions in 10-K filings to measure the existence of trade secrets. Specifically, Trade Secret is defined as a dummy variable that equals 1 if the firms' 10-K filings in the past three years mention "trade secret" or "trade secrecy."$^{15}$ R&D is measured as R&D expenditure over total assets. Missing R&D expenditures are coded as zero. To address the issue that firms with missing R&D expenditures could have significant R&D investments (Koh and Reeb 2015), we also include an indicator variable for missing R&D expenditures,

denoted as Miss R&D. Table 1 Panel B reports that an average firm has 32 patent filings in a year (Patents, untransformed). The mean of Trade Secret is 0.829, suggesting that 82.9% of firm-years discuss trade secrecy in the 10-Ks in the past three years. R&D has a mean of 0.064 and a median of 0.012; 33.1% of firms have missing R&D expenditure data.

#### 5.1.2 Cybersecurity threats

It is unclear whether GAI would reduce or exacerbate a firm's cybersecurity threats because it can lead to both benefits and risks in cybersecurity (Campbell 2023; Irei 2023). Regarding the benefits, GAI can increase protection efficiency, improve threat detection, automate responses, and continuously monitor (Campbell 2023). However, GAI can also help attackers launch sophisticated and novel attacks. For example, ChatGPT can be used to write malware and help hackers build their skills (Irei 2023). $ ^{16} $ In addition, employees using GAI tools like ChatGPT could put sensitive corporate information at risk. $ ^{17} $ For these reasons, it is unclear whether firms with higher or lower cybersecurity threats are affected more by GAI regarding their cybersecurity and in which direction.

We assess cybersecurity threats by evaluating the likelihood that a firm might face cyberattacks using an approach based on Florackis et al. (2023). Specifically, we compare a firm's description of its cybersecurity vulnerabilities in the Item 1a. Risk Factors section of its 10-K filings against the characteristics of all documented past cyberattacks of firms. A higher overlap (similarity) between the focal firm's cybersecurity risk disclosure and traits of actual past cyberattacks suggests a higher possibility of real threats.

We gather information on cybersecurity incidents from the Privacy Rights Clearinghouse’s (PRC) Data Breach Chronology Database, focusing exclusively on incidents related to cyberattacks or hacking. The PRC database provides extensive details on each incident, such as the nature of the information compromised and the incident’s magnitude. We compile the descriptions of these incidents to create a reference point for evaluating real cybersecurity threats. Next we extract statements related to cybersecurity from the section Item 1a. Risk Factors of a firm’s 10-K filings. We identify relevant disclosures by searching for specific keywords, as suggested by Florackis et al. (2023), such as “cyberattack,” “data breaches,” and “data theft.” We collect all sentences containing these keywords as cybersecurity disclosures for each firm-year. Lastly, we calculate the cosine similarity between the text of the firm’s disclosures and the descriptions of real past cyber incidents in the PRC dataset, denoted as CS Threat. A higher value suggests that the company’s disclosures closely match the details of actual cyberattacks, indicating a higher vulnerability. Table 1 Panel B reports that CS Threat has a mean and a median of 0.691 and 0.695, respectively, sug

gesting that 69.1% of an average firm’s cybersecurity-threat disclosures in 10-K filings resemble the actual incidents from the PRC Data Breach Chronology Database.

#### 5.1.3 Labor exposure to AI

GAI is widely perceived to have great potential to substitute for labor and increase labor productivity (McKinsey 2023; Yilmaz et al. 2023; Boyaci et al. 2024; Brynjolfsson et al. 2025). However, what is less obvious is that GAI might also hurt labor productivity and firm performance. Many employees have concerns about job insecurity, which could hurt their psychological health, lessen organizational commitment, and decrease their human capital investment (Dekker and Schaufeli 1995; Brougham and Haar 2018). To investigate how managers view the influence of GAI on firms through its effect on labor, we examine how managers of firms whose labor is more affected by AI (higher labor exposure to AI) change their GAI-related discussions in conference calls relative to other firms after ChatGPT's release. Given the expected impact of GAI on labor, we expect that managers of firms with higher AI exposure are more likely to increase GAI-related discussions. However, based on the two-sided arguments above, it remains unclear whether their perspectives tend to be positive or negative and whether they are more likely to discuss concrete actions.

We measure a firm’s labor force exposure to AI using the AI Industry Exposure (AIIE) developed by Felten et al. (2021). $ ^{18} $ Felten et al. (2021) identify a set of common and well-developed applications of AI, relate these to abilities in workplace activities of each occupation, and develop a measure of an occupation’s exposure to AI technologies, which they call AI Occupational Exposure (AOE). They validate their measure in several ways, for example, by showing that AIOE is positively associated with use of AI skills within an occupation based on the job postings. Due to the lack of firm-level occupation data for the construction of a firm-level AI exposure measure, we rely on their measure, which is constructed by aggregating AIOEs across occupations within an industry (AIIE). This means that we focus on between-industry variation of labor exposure to AI. We denote this measure as AIIE. The mean of AIIE in our sample is 0.642, suggesting that on average 64.2% occupational abilities relate to AI applications at the industry level (Table 1 Panel B).

#### 5.1.4 Customer operations

While GAI is generally believed to significantly impact customer operations, it is unclear whether it will always improve them. On one hand, GAI may revolutionize the entire customer operations function (BCG 2023; Siggelkow and Terwiesch 2023; Brynjolfsson et al. 2025). On the other, using GAI in customer services also presents risks. For instance, it can provide false or biased information, alienate different customers, and lack the compassion and empathy of a live customer agent, which could harm customer loyalty (BCG 2023; Froehlich 2023). We anticipate that managers of firms with greater customer operations will likely intensify their discussions about

GAI more. Nevertheless, it remains uncertain whether their viewpoints are predominantly positive or negative and whether they are more likely to discuss actions.

Due to the lack of individual customer data, we also rely on the industry-level variation to measure customer operations. $ ^{19} $ We posit that firms in the retail and services industries have greater customer operations than those in other industries (Verma 2000; Wang 2024). We identify retail and services industries based on two-digit SIC industry codes. Specifically, we classify the retail industry by two-digit SIC codes 52–59 and the service industry by two-digit SIC codes 72, 73, and 78 (Cole and Jones 2004). We define a variable Customer that equals one if a firm is in a retail or services industry and zero otherwise. Its mean is 0.204 in our sample, indicating that 20.4% of observations are in retail and service industries (Table 1 Panel B).

### 5.2 Regression analyses

#### 5.2.1 Determinants of changes in managers' GAI discussion intensity

To examine how the four firm and industry characteristics of interest are associated with changes in managers’ GAI discussion intensity in conference calls surrounding the release of ChatGPT, we run a regression of  $ \Delta GAI\% $ on the measures of these characteristics, separately and collectively:

 $$ \Delta GAI\%_{i,t}=\alpha+\beta X_{i,t-1}+\lambda Controls_{i,t-1}+\varepsilon_{i,t}, $$ 

where the independent variable of interest,  $ X_{i,t-1} $, is Patents, Trade Secret, measures of R&D expenditure (R&D and Miss R&D), CS Threat, AIIE, or Customer. Controls refers to control variables. We first control for the fundamental firm and industry characteristics we examine in Sect. 3, including Firm Size, Market to Book, Firm Age, and High Tech Industry—an indicator for the high-tech industries. As AIIE and Customer are measured at the industry level, we do not include industry fixed effects. Studies find that firms with greater investor interest are more likely to host conference calls (Tasker 1998; Frankel et al. 1999). To control for investor information demand, we include Analyst Coverage, measured by the number of financial analysts following a firm, and Institutional Shareholding, measured by the percentage of shares outstanding owned by institutional investors. As studies find that firms with less return volatility provide more voluntary disclosures (Li 2010), we also control for Abnormal Return Volatility, measured by the standard deviation of market-adjusted return. We winsorize continuous variables at the 1 percent and 99 percent levels and cluster the standard errors at the industry (three-digit SIC) level.

Table 3 Panel A presents the estimation results. To facilitate interpretation, in this and all subsequent analyses, we normalize  $ \Delta GAI\% $, Patents, R&D, CS Threat, and AIIE by subtracting the sample mean and then dividing by the standard deviation. In Column (1), we include only the control variables to estimate a benchmark model as

well as to examine how the fundamental firm and industry characteristics examined in Sects. 3.4.2 and 3.4.3 are associated with  $ \Delta GAI\% $ in a multivariate regression. Consistent with the univariate evidence in Sects. 3.4.2 and 3.4.3, Size, Market to Book, and High Tech Industry load positively and significantly, with a coefficient of 0.04 (t-stat=2.74), 0.01 (t-stat=2.64), and 0.29 (t-stat=1.93), respectively, and Firm Age loads negatively and significantly, with a coefficient of -0.04 (t-stat=-2.24). These coefficients are also economically significant. A one standard deviation increase in Size and Market to Book corresponds to a 0.08 and 0.14 standard deviation increase in  $ \Delta GAI\% $, while a one standard deviation increase in Firm Age corresponds to a 0.80 standard deviation decrease in  $ \Delta GAI\% $. Compared with other industries, a high-tech industry firm has a 0.29 standard deviation increase in  $ \Delta GAI\% $. These results suggest that managers of larger, younger, growth, and high-tech firms are more likely to increase GAI discussions.

In Columns (2)–(7), the six measures of innovation, cybersecurity threats, labor exposure to AI, and customer operations are included individually, and in Column (8), they are included simultaneously. We find that all measures, except the measures of trade secrets and R&D expenditures, load positively and significantly, both individually and collectively. When all variables are included (Column (8)), Patents has a coefficient of 0.06 (t-stat=2.30), CS Threat of 0.05 (t-stat=2.25), AIIE of 0.07 (t-stat=2.87), and Customer of 0.51 (t-stat=3.94). These coefficients are also economically significant. A one standard deviation increase of Patents corresponds to a 0.06 standard deviation increase in  $ \Delta GAI\% $. As the mean and standard deviation of  $ \Delta GAI\% $ are 0.306 and 1.609, respectively, this effect accounts for a 32% increase ( $ =0.06 \times 1.609/0.306 $) of the sample mean. A similar calculation suggests that a one standard deviation increase in CS Threat (AIIE) corresponds to an increase of  $ \Delta GAI\% $ that accounts for 26% (37%) of its sample mean. As Customer is an indicator variable, its coefficient 0.51 indicates that  $ \Delta GAI\% $ is larger by 0.51 standard deviation in industries with high customer operations (retail and services industries) than others, which accounts for 268% ( $ =0.51 \times 1.609/0.306 $) of the sample mean of  $ \Delta GAI\% $. $ ^{20} $ In addition, Size, Market to Book, Firm Age, and High Tech Industry continue to load significantly and consistently across the specifications. Overall, the results in Table 3 Panel A indicate that the increase in managers' GAI-related discussions in conference calls after the release of ChatGPT is greater for larger, younger, growth, and high-tech firms as well as firms with more patent filings, greater cybersecurity threats, higher labor exposure to AI, and more customer operations, suggesting that these firms are perceived by managers to be more impacted by GAI.

The different findings for patents, trade secrets, and R&D expenditures suggest that the association between innovation intensity and changes of managers' GAI discussions is subtle. While patents and trade secrets can both protect innovations, the latter afford weaker protection (Hall et al. 2014). A trade secret may be legally appropriated by a firm that has independently discovered or reverse engineered it, whereas a patent may not. Hence, firms with more patents may view GAI as present

ing greater opportunities to innovate without fearing appropriation by competitors. $ ^{21} $ R&D expenditure, on the other hand, captures innovation inputs whose outcome is highly uncertain, and thus how firms with higher R&D expenditures are affected by GAI is unclear.

For comparison, we also investigate changes of analysts’ GAI discussions using  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Analyst as the dependent variable and report the results in Table 3 Panel B. We find that financial analysts increase GAI discussions more for firms with greater cybersecurity threats, higher labor exposure to AI, and more customer operations, like the evidence for managers. However, unlike the evidence for managers, the association between  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Analyst and Patents is insignificant. As analysts’ questions follow managers’ presentations, those discussions reflect their perceptions and demand for additional information. $ ^{22} $ The insignificant result for Patents suggests that analysts do not demand more information about GAI for firms with more patents after managers’ discussions. Overall, the evidence suggests that analysts’ perceptions are largely consistent with those of the managers.

#### 5.2.2 Changes of managers' GAI discussions with different sentiment

While the analysis in Table 3 speaks to which types of firms’ managers perceive their firms to be more affected by GAI, the directions of the impacts are unclear. To address this question, we examine how the firm and industry characteristics are associated with changes of managers’ GAI discussions with positive, negative, and neutral tones. We re-estimate Eq. (1) using  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Pos,  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Neg, and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Neu as the dependent variables. The findings are reported in Table 4 Panel A. Again, we normalize the dependent variables and Patents, R&D, CS Threat, and AIIE. This makes the comparison across regressions not confounded by the relative frequencies of GAI discussions of different tones. $ ^{23} $

The coefficients of Patents and CS Threat are positive and significant for  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Pos as the dependent variable (Column (1)), while they are insignificant for  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Neg (Column (2)) and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Neu (Column (3)). Furthermore, the coefficients in the Column (1) are significantly more positive than the corresponding ones in Column (2). These results indicate that, for firms with more patent filings or cybersecurity threats, managers increase positive-tone GAI discussions more but not negative- or neutral-tone discussions, suggesting that managers view GAI's impact on these firms to be positive. This table reports the regression results of the determinants of changes in managers' discussions of GAI after the launch of ChatGPT (in Panel A) as well as those of analysts (in Panel B) based on the discussion sentiment (positive, negative, or neutral). Variable definitions are in Appendix D. The dependent variable and key non-indicator independent variables Patents, R&D, CS Threat, and AIIE are standardized. t-statistics (in parentheses) are based on standard errors clustered at the industry level. ***, **, and * indicate two-sided significance at the 1%, 5%, and 10% levels, respectively.

tures are insignificant in all regressions. Thus, trade secrets and R&D expenditures are unassociated not only with overall changes of GAI discussion intensity but also those of specific tones.

The coefficients on AIIE and Customer are positive and significant in all columns, suggesting that managers of firms with greater labor exposure to AI and more customer operations are more likely to increase GAI discussions of all tones. However, the coefficients are significantly more positive for positive-tone discussions (Column (1)) than for negative-tone ones (Column (2)), which suggests that, for firms with higher AIIE and Customer, managers' perceptions of GAI's impact are more likely to be positive.

These results suggest that managers of firms with more patents and firms facing greater cybersecurity threats view GAI positively, while the views of managers of firms with greater labor exposure to AI and firms with more customer operations are more varied, though they are more likely to be positive than negative. We again report the results based on analysts' GAI discussions in Panel B for comparison. The results are consistent with those for managers in Panel A, except that Patents is insignificantly associated with changes in positive-tone analyst discussions. This comparison resembles that in Table 3.

To examine the three types of discussions together, we construct a broad measure of positive-tone GAI discussions by combining GAI%-Pos, GAI%-Neg, and GAI%-Neu using the weighting scheme 1, -1, and 0. Specifically, for each conference call, we define GAI%-Pos-Broad as GAI%-Pos minus GAI%-Neg. We then calculate the change of this composite measure for each firm after the launch of ChatGPT, denoted as  $ \Delta GAI\%-Pos-Broad $. We use this variable as the dependent variable and estimate Eq. (1). As reported in Table IA-3 of the internet appendix, we continue to find that Patents, CS Threat, AIIE, and Customer are positively associated with changes of the broadly defined positive-tone discussions.

#### 5.2.3 Changes of managers' GAI discussions with and without initiatives

When discussing GAI, managers may disclose initiatives or comment broadly. For example, Balakrishnan et al. (2023) find that, when managers discuss racial diversity

issues in conference calls, some mention specific actions, such as appointing Black directors, while others offer general remarks but no plans. Thus, we further investigate how the firm and industry characteristics we study are associated with the changes of managers' GAI discussions with and without initiatives. Using  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Initiative and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-NonInitiative as dependent variables, we re-estimate Eq. (1) and report the results in Table 5 Panel A. As in Tables 3 and 4, we normalize the dependent variables and Patents, R&D, CS Threat, and AIIE. The coefficients on Patents, CS Threat, and Customer are significantly positive when  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Initiative is the dependent variable (Column (1)) but insignificant when  $ \Delta GAI\% $-NonInitiative is the dependent variable (Column (2)). Moreover, the difference between each pair of corresponding coefficients is significant. These results suggest that, for firms with more patent filings, greater cybersecurity threats, and more customer operations, managers increase GAI discussions with initiatives more after the release of ChatGPT, while there are no such associations for discussions without initiatives.

The coefficients of  $ AIIE $ are significantly positive in both columns, with the one in Column (1) significantly exceeding that in Column (2). This result suggests that managers of firms with greater labor exposure to AI increase discussions of both types more after the release of ChatGPT but the increase is greater for initiative-related discussions. As in Tables 3 and 4, trade secrets and R&D expenditures are insignificantly associated with changes in both types of discussion. The results for analyst discussions reported in Panel B are consistent with those for managers, except that Patents relates insignificantly to changes in analyst initiative discussions.

### 5.3 AI washing?

One concern may be that managers’ GAI discussions in conference calls reflect AI washing. We provide three pieces of evidence that alleviate these concerns. First, we categorize the discussions along four nonmutually exclusive dimensions using Chat-GPT-4 (guided by and verified with manual reading). Eight-seven percent of GAI initiative discussions entail actions that have already been taken; 74% of discussions are specific; 38% entail partnership with a GAI vendor; and 68% are with sufficient certainty. Considering the significant legal liabilities for managers to mislead investors in conference calls, these features are inconsistent with AI washing. Second, we ask ChatGPT-4 to read all initiative-related discussions to determine whether they are likely AI washing. $ ^{24} $ Only nine out of 312 calls (2.9%) are identified as AI washing. Twenty-one calls (6.7%) are identified as “not sure,” and the remaining 90.4% are identified as not AI washing. $ ^{25} $

This table reports the regression results of changes in managers' discussion of GAI after the launch of ChatGPT (in Panel A) as well as those of analysts (in Panel B) on innovation intensity, cybersecurity threats, product differentiation, labor, and customer operations, respectively, based on the discussion content (initiative versus non-initiative). See Appendix D for variable definitions. The dependent variables  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Initiative and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-NonInitiative and key non-indicator independent variables Patents, R&D, CS Threat, and AIIE are standardized. Panel A focuses on managers' discussions, whereas Panel B focuses on analysts' discussions. t-statistics (in parentheses) are based on standard errors clustered at the industry level (SIC3). ***, **, and * indicate two-sided significance at the 1%, 5%, and 10% levels, respectively.

Finally, we posit that, because AI talent is crucial to firms' AI strategy, if firms truly undertake GAI initiatives, they are likely to hire more AI talent. To test this conjecture, we compare AI-related job postings of GAI discussing and nondiscussing firms during the year after ChatGPT's launch using the Revelio Labs workforce database. We identify AI-related jobs based on manual coding of the job classifications. We report the results in Table 6. Among firms discussing GAI, an average of 365 AI-related jobs are posted, significantly exceeding the 103 AI-related jobs posted, on average, by firms that do not discuss GAI. Moreover, the percentage of AI postings relative to total postings is significantly higher for firms that discuss GAI compared to those that do not. The inferences are similar when we compare firms with initiative-related GAI discussions and those without them, with the differences slightly larger. Taken together, our evidence mitigates the concern that managers' GAI discussions may primarily reflect AI washing. However, we acknowledge that we cannot rule out this concern and leave the further exploration of this issue for future research.

This table reports firms’ AI-related job postings in one year after the launch of ChatGPT (from December 2022 to November 2023) for firms with GAI discussions, firms with initiative-related GAI discussions, and firms without discussions.  $ * $ $ * $ $ * $,  $ * $ $ * $, and  $ * $ indicate two-sided significance at the 1%, 5%, and 10% levels, respectively

## 6 Market reactions to changes in managers' GAI discussions

In this section, we examine market reactions to managers’ GAI discussions in conference calls to assess whether and how the discussions inform investors. We measure market reactions with the absolute value of abnormal returns ( $ |Call\ CAR| $) and abnormal trading volume (Ab. Volume) over the two-day window  $ [0,+1] $, where day 0 is the conference call dates. We regress  $ |Call\ CAR| $ and Ab. Volume on  $ \Delta GAI\% $ and a set of control variables measured at the closest fiscal quarter (e.g., Bushee et al. 2011; Frankel et al. 1999) and report the results in Table 7. $ ^{26} $ We find that changes in managers’ GAI discussions are significantly and positively associated with both  $ |Call\ CAR| $ and Ab. Volume (Columns (1) and (2) in Panel A). Specifically, the coefficient for  $ \Delta GAI\% $ is 0.03 for abnormal returns and 0.05 for abnormal trading volume, both significant at the 1% level. We find similar results for  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Pos-Broad (Columns (3) and (4)), the change of the broad measure of positive-tone discussions defined in Sect. 4.2.2, and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Initiative (Columns (5) and (6)). These results suggest that changes of managers’ GAI discussions (intensity and features) do inform investors.

Next we examine whether the market reactions vary with the level of institutional ownership, to assess whether institutional or retail investors drive these responses. We categorize firms into two groups: high and low institutional shareholding, based on the highest and lowest sample tercile. The results presented in Table 7 Panels B and C show that market reactions are primarily concentrated among firms with high institutional shareholding. While both groups react to  $ \Delta GAI\% $ and  $ \Delta GAI\% $. Initiative, only the high institutional shareholding group reacts significantly to  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Pos-Broad. The coefficients for firms with high institutional ownership significantly exceed those for the low institutional ownership group, except for  $ \Delta GAI\% $-NonInitiative. Overall, these findings suggest that institutional investors drive the market responses.

Finally, to explore the possibility that initiative-related discussions with distinct features may have varying content, we explore whether the market reacts differently to changes of initiative-related GAI discussions with different features discussed in the first point of Sect. 4.3 (e.g., specificity and whether the action has occurred) and report the results in Table 7 Panel D. For initiatives that have occurred versus those that have not, the reactions reflected in  $ \left|\mathrm{Call~CAR}\right| $ are both significant and have similar magnitudes. However, the reaction based on Ab. Volume is significant only for the former. The market reacts significantly to discussions of specific initiatives and those with sufficient certainty, based on both measures, whereas the reactions are insignificant for vague initiatives and those without sufficient certainty. The market reactions to initiatives involving a partnership with a GAI vendor do not differ significantly from those to initiatives not mentioning a partnership. Overall, we find that initiative-related GAI discussions have greater information content when they are more specific and more certain.

word-wrap: break-word;'>$ 0.05^{***} $</td></tr><tr><td colspan="9">This table reports the regression of the market-adjusted abnormal returns (in absolute value) and abnormal trading volume to conference calls and managers&#x27; GA discussions, over the two-day window  $ [0, +1] $, where day 0 refers to the conference call date. Panel A reports the full sample results. Panel B (C) constrains sample to firm whose retail trading is in the lowest (highest) tercile. Panel D reports the results for changes of initiative-related GA1 discussions with different features. The depender variables and key non-indicator independent variable  $ AGAP_{1} $,  $ AGAP_{2} $-Pos-Broad,  $ AGAP_{2} $-Initiative, and  $ AGAP_{2} $-NonInitiative are standardized. Variable definitions are in Appendix D. t-statistics (in the parentheses right below the coefficient estimates) are based on standard errors clustered at the industry level. In Panel C, we also report the p-value of testing the difference of the coefficient with the corresponding coefficient in Panel B in the square parenthesis below the t-statistic. ***, **, and * indicate two-sided significance at the 1%, 5%, and 10% levels, respectively.</td></tr></table>

## 7 Additional analyses

### 7.1 Robustness tests

In all regression analyses above, we use the change of GAI discussions or those with certain content features (sentiment and initiative-relatedness) as the dependent variable to examine managers' responses to the release of ChatGPT. To understand how managers and analysts discuss GAI cross-sectionally in the post-ChatGPT period, we use the level of discussions (e.g., GAI% and GAI%-Pos) as the dependent variable and repeat the analyses in Tables 3, 4 and 5. As reported in Table 8 Panel A, the results are generally consistent with those in Tables 3, 4 and 5. Thus, all our conclusions regarding changes in managers' GAI discussions hold for the level of discussions in the post-ChatGPT period. This is expected because managers rarely discuss GAI before ChatGPT. Next we conduct a robustness test for the analyses in Tables 3, 4 and 5 by measuring GAI discussions based on the number of words (as opposed to sentences) relative to the total number of words in managers' disclosures in a call. The results reported in Table 8 Panel B are also consistent with those in Tables 3, 4 and 5.

### 7.2 Within-industry analyses

We do not include industry fixed effects in Eq. (1) because AIIE and Customer are measured at the industry level. Thus, within- and between-industry variations both drive our findings regarding innovation intensity and cybersecurity threats. To explore whether within-industry variation in these three factors have similar effects, we incorporate industry (three-digit SIC) fixed effects in Eq. (1). The findings are detailed in Table 9. For managers' GAI discussions in Panel A, Patents and CS Threat continue to have significant and positive coefficients for  $ \Delta GAI\% $,  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Pos, and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Initiative. For analysts' GAI discussions in Panel B, CS Threat continues to have a significant and positive coefficient for  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Analyst,  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Pos-Analyst, and  $ \Delta GAI\% $-Initiative-Analyst. These results suggest that within-industry variation in innovation (Patents) and cybersecurity threats also contributes to the managers' results above and within-industry variation of cybersecurity threats also contributes to the analysts' results above.

### 7.3 Market reaction to launch of ChatGPT

Finally, we explore whether managers’ perceptions of GAI’s impact are consistent with those of investors by examining market reaction to the launch of ChatGPT on November 30, 2022. We measure the market reaction by the cumulative abnormal return over the two-day window  $ [0,+1] $, where day 0 is the ChatGPT launch date (GPT Launch CAR) and regress the market reaction on the measures of firm and industry characteristics of interest. The results are reported in Table 10. In Columns (1)–(6), we include one measure at a time, and in Column (7), we include all measures. Consistent with our findings from managers’ GAI discussions, Patents and CS Threat are positively and significantly associated with GPT Launch CAR, with

This table reports the regression results of the determinants of market reactions to the launch of ChatGPT on November 30, 2022. Variable definitions are in Appendix D. t-statistics (in parentheses) are based on standard errors clustered at the industry level.  $ ^{***} $,  $ ^{**} $, and  $ ^{*} $ indicate two-sided significance at the 1%, 5%, and 10% levels, respectively.

coefficients of 0.19 (t-stat=3.15) and 3.45 (t-stat=2.25), respectively. This result suggests that investors perceive GAI to benefit firms with more patents and greater cybersecurity threats.

However, unlike our findings based on managers’ GAI discussions, Trade Secret and R&D are negatively and significantly associated with GPT Launch CAR, with coefficients of  $ -0.83 $ (t-stat=-2.25) and  $ -4.52 $ (t-stat=-10.09), respectively, while both AIIE and Customer do not load significantly. $ ^{27} $ Thus, it seems that investors perceive GAI to hurt firms with higher R&D expenditures and those with more trade secrets, while their perception of GAI’s impact does not vary with labor exposure to AI or customer operations. As we discuss in Sect. 4, GAI’s impacts on firms with different levels of innovation intensity, labor exposure to AI, and customer operations are unclear. The results in Table 10 suggest that, along these dimensions, managers’ perceptions seem to be more positive than those of investors. These inconsistencies are understandable because ChatGPT is a breakthrough technology and its impacts on business are uncertain. Thus, investors’ perceptions, reflected in price changes within several days, could disagree with those of managers. In this case, examining the perceptions and responses of various economic agents (investors, managers, analysts, and others) can better depict GAI’s impacts.

GAI announcement returns may also be confounded by other important market-wide events occurring on November 30, 2022. For example, on that same day, Federal Reserve Chair Jerome Powell delivered a speech indicating the possibility of slowing

the pace of interest rate hikes, and the Commerce Department released a higher-than-expected GDP growth rate. While we adjust our return measure for the market return, the impacts of confounding events could vary with the firm and industry characteristics we study, contaminating our return analysis. Note that these confounding events do not contaminate our analyses of conference calls because we specifically examine GAI discussions. Moreover, calls may release other new information that we cannot control for, so our findings should be interpreted with caution.

## 8 Conclusion

We provide early evidence of firms' responses to GAI through managerial discussions of GAI in conference calls after the release of ChatGPT in November 2022. We find that, following the release, these managerial discussions increase substantially and most discussions have positive tones and contain action initiatives. The increase is more pronounced for larger firms, growth firms, younger firms, and those in high-tech industries. The largest increase is observed in the Fama–French-12 industry of business equipment—computers, software, and electronic equipment—while the utilities, chemicals and allied products, and oil, gas, and coal extraction industries experience the smallest increases. We further examine how the change in managers' GAI discussions is associated with several important firm and industry characteristics related to the major ways GAI is believed to impact firms, including innovation, cybersecurity, labor, and customers. We find that managers of firms with greater innovation intensity (reflected in patent filings), cybersecurity threats, labor exposure to AI, and customer operations are more likely to increase GAI discussions.

Our analysis of discussion sentiment suggests that managers of firms with more patent filings and those with greater cybersecurity threats hold positive views of GAI. While managers of firms with greater labor exposure to AI and those with more customer operations hold mixed views, their perspectives are more likely to be positive than negative. Additional analysis suggests that managers of firms with more patent filings, greater cybersecurity threats, and more customer operations increase initiative-related discussions more but not discussions without initiatives. While managers of firms with greater labor exposure to AI increase discussions of both types more, they are more likely to increase initiative-related ones. We also find that investors, especially institutional investors, find changes of managers' GAI discussions in conference calls following the launch of ChatGPT informative.

We find similar results for changes in analysts’ GAI discussions in conference calls, except that analysts’ responses generally do not vary with firms’ patent filings. Exploring market reactions to the launch of ChatGPT, we find that investors’ perceptions are consistent with managers in some but not all dimensions. Overall, our study illuminates the heterogeneous corporate responses to GAI.
